Fourier-enhanced TecoGAN: advancing video super-resolution with spectral and gradient losses

Md. Asif Hasan, Radee Jamil Khan — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1007/s11042-026-21990-4

Abstract Video super-resolution (VSR) aims to reconstruct high-resolution (HR) video frames from low-resolution (LR) inputs by exploiting both spatial details and temporal dependencies across frames. The TecoGAN framework introduced adversarial training with spatio-temporal discriminators and a novel Ping-Pong loss to generate perceptually realistic and temporally coherent videos. However, TecoGAN’s generator architecture relies on transposed convolutions, which can introduce checkerboard artifacts, and has limited capacity to recover fine high-frequency details. In this paper, we propose Fourier-Enhanced TecoGAN , an improved GAN-based VSR framework that addresses these limitations through architectural and loss-function enhancements. Specifically, we adopt Residual-in-Residual Dense Blocks (RRDBs) from ESRGAN to strengthen feature extraction, and replace transposed convolution upsampling with PixelShuffle layers to reduce artifacts. We further introduce a frequency-aware loss that operates in the Fourier domain to emphasize high-frequency reconstruction, along with a gradient-magnitude loss to better preserve edges and structural details. To improve training stability, we employ a hinge-based adversarial loss and apply spectral normalization to the discriminator. Experimental results on the REDS dataset demonstrate that the proposed method consistently outperforms the original TecoGAN, achieving improved PSNR and SSIM while reducing LPIPS by approximately 20% and lowering temporal optical flow error (tOF). Qualitative results further show sharper textures and improved temporal stability compared to prior state-of-the-art VSR methods. Code and pretrained models will be released upon publication.

The NIKA2 Cosmological Legacy Survey in COSMOS: Final 1.2,mm and 2,mm source catalogs and redshift distribution of dusty star-forming galaxies

Cristian Carvajal-Bohorquez, G. Lagache, A. Beelen, R. Adam, P. Ade — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1051/0004-6361/202660707

Dusty star-forming galaxies (DSFGs) play a major role in the overall star formation in the Universe at high redshift. While deep near-infrared surveys, particularly with JWST, can now identify many heavily obscured galaxies, (sub)millimeter observations are essential for measuring their dust emission and quantifying their dust content and obscured star formation. We present the final 1.2,mm and 2,mm source catalogs and the redshift distribution of the millimeter-selected population from the NIKA2 Cosmological Legacy Survey (N2CLS) in the COSMOS field. Our aim was to provide a comprehensive dataset for studying the physical properties and evolution of high-redshift DSFGs and to compare the observed redshift distribution with theoretical predictions. We analyzed deep NIKA2 observations at 1.2,mm and 2,mm, covering ∼1070,arcmin2$ with median noise levels of 315,mathrm μJy and 91,mathrm μJy, respectively. Sources were extracted with a signal-to-noise ratio (S/N) threshold of 3.9, ensuring ≥80% purity. We identified multiwavelength counterparts using high-resolution interferometric (sub)millimeter data (NOEMA, ALMA) and radio observations (VLA, MeerKAT). Redshifts were compiled from spectroscopic and photometric catalogs (e.g., COSMOSWeb). We compared the redshift distribution to predictions from the SIDES simulations and other galaxy evolution models (e.g., SHARK and FLAMINGO). The N2CLS master catalog includes 323 sources detected at ≥80% purity, with 104 sources detected in both bands, 197 only at 1.2,mm, and 22 only at 2,mm. Multiwavelength identifications were secured for ∼89% of the sample. The redshift distribution of 1.2,mm high-purity (geq95%) sources peaks at $2.8 ± 0.1, consistent with the epoch of peak cosmic star formation. In the total sample, we lack redshifts for sim2% of the identified galaxies plus 34 sources for which no accurate positional proxy is available, preventing the identification of a multiwavelength counterpart. We identified 66 galaxies at z > 4 (19 with spectroscopic redshifts). The observed redshift distribution agrees well with the SIDES simulations, while four other galaxy evolution models are statistically inconsistent with the data. Using NIKA2 at the IRAM 30,m telescope, we conducted N2CLS, the largest contiguous deep millimeter survey to date at this depth with homogeneous coverage. This homogeneous coverage is important, as 25% of N2CLS sources lack a SCUBA2 850,̊m μ m counterpart, likely because the strongly nonuniform noise distribution of the SCUBA2 map results in lower sensitivity in parts of the field. The released data products, including maps, catalogs, and multiwavelength identifications, provide a legacy dataset for studying dust-obscured galaxy evolution. The N2CLS bridges wide-area single-dish observations and high-resolution interferometric studies, enabling future investigations into the physical properties and cosmic evolution of DSFGs.

Development and validation of a viscosity-equivalence framework for rejuvenator dosage optimization in RAP binders using waste engine oil and waste transformer oil

M. Surendranath, M. Sivakumar — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.nxmate.2026.103974

Optimizing the rejuvenator dosage is critical for restoring the workability and performance of reclaimed asphalt pavement (RAP) binders. However, existing dosage selection approaches are generally based on individual engineering properties or viscosity measured at one or a few temperatures, resulting in property-specific optimum dosages and overlooking viscosity behavior across the practical mixing and compaction temperature range. This study proposes and validates a viscosity-equivalence-based framework to determine the optimum dosage of waste engine oil (WEO) and waste transformer oil (WTO) for RAP binders. The framework employs viscosity-ratio (VR) analysis to establish viscosity equivalence between rejuvenated RAP binders and the control binder over the practical mixing and compaction temperature range. The framework identified 12.5% WEO and 8.5% WTO as the optimum dosages achieving strict viscosity equivalence (VR ≈ 1.0), while 7.5% WTO was retained alongside 12.5% WEO as a conservative evaluation baseline. Downstream validation confirmed that both target formulations restored penetration, softening point, and continuous high PG to levels statistically equivalent to the virgin control binder (p ≥ 0.23). Spectral deconvolution verified partial chemical de-aging, diluting IC=O (Carbonyl Index) and IS=O (Sulfoxide Index) indices by 51–73% (p < 0.001) while remaining above control binder levels. Rheological testing on unaged blends demonstrated enhanced rutting resistance with 21.4–26.2% lower non-recoverable compliance (J nr,3.2 ) than the control binder (p < 0.001), meeting AASHTO M 332 Standard traffic criteria. Concurrently, intermediate fatigue life (N f at 25 °C) expanded by 4.57 × (WTO) and 15.0 × (WEO) over the control binder (p < 0.001), with WEO exhibiting 3.29 × higher endurance than WTO (p < 0.001). The proposed framework provides a systematic, reproducible methodology for optimizing rejuvenator dosage and can be extended to other rejuvenator systems following appropriate experimental validation.

TACO: An open, interpretable model for microsecond-scale TCR specificity prediction

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Marco Garcia Noceda, Erin Calfee, Brian C. Zhang, Edward J. Osborne, Brad J. Greenfield — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.11.04.764339

Predicting antigen-induced T-cell activation from T-cell receptor (TCR) and peptide sequence remains difficult for TCRs and peptides not represented in training data. We present TACO (T-Cell Activation from Chemistry and Orientation), an open statistical model for predicting TCR specificity against HLA-A*02:01-presented peptides. TACO takes as input the peptide sequence and six TCR CDR loop sequences. Learning from binary activation labels, TACO selects a single canonical orientation of the CDR loops over the peptide and learns five amino-acid contact-potential matrices as well as two peptide-exposure profiles.The orientation is fixed and does not require experimentally determined or predicted structures. We evaluate TACO on held-out peptides separated by Levenshtein distance at least four from all training and validation peptides, limiting near-neighbor shortcut learning. TACO achieves AUROC 0.67 on a large held-out activation dataset and 0.68 on IMMREP25 HLA-A*02:01, exceeding the open structure-based models evaluated here and matching the reported AlphaFold3-TCRdock result. The model has 2,019 fitted parameters, retains most within-peptide ranking signal with TCR$\beta$ sequence alone, and reaches 406,000 TCR--peptide predictions per second on CPU. This throughput enables computational evaluation of large TCR-peptide search spaces. We show its potential use for prioritizing candidate target peptides from large peptide libraries, reducing the search space for downstream experimental investigation. TACO fitted parameters also expose candidate physicochemical patterns that can be inspected directly. We release TACO's parameters and complete training, evaluation, and inference code.

Integrative multi-omics, machine learning, computational toxicology, and experimental validation reveal PFAS-associated immune–inflammatory signatures in chronic obstructive pulmonary disease

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Zhengyu Hu, Yuanlan Yang, Jialin Yu, Miaomiao Nian, Jieda Cui — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fimmu.2026.1965629

Background Per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) are persistent environmental contaminants increasingly implicated in respiratory disorders. However, the systems-level connections among PFAS-associated molecular perturbations, immune dysregulation, and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) remain poorly understood. This study aimed to prioritize candidate links between PFAS-associated target information and COPD-related transcriptional and inflammatory signatures. Methods PFAS-associated target information was integrated with bulk transcriptomic differential expression and weighted gene co-expression network analysis to identify overlapping candidate genes. Protein–protein interaction analysis and 113 machine-learning model combinations were used for candidate prioritization and cross-cohort classification evaluation. CIBERSORT and xCell provided computational immune-cell signatures, with Benjamini–Hochberg correction applied to group comparisons and gene–signature correlations; single-cell transcriptomics provided cell-type expression context. Candidate expression was examined in cigarette-smoke-extract-treated cells, clinical samples, and public human and murine datasets. Molecular docking and molecular dynamics simulations explored candidate PFAS–protein interactions. Results HMOX1, JUNB, CXCL12, and PTGS2 were prioritized as candidate genes overlapping PFAS-associated target information and COPD-related expression patterns. Enrichment and immune-deconvolution analyses suggested associations with inflammatory and immune-related signatures, while single-cell analysis described their distribution across annotated cell types. Exploratory plasma ELISA measurements supported higher levels of the four candidate proteins in participants with COPD, with additional expression support varying by gene and experimental model. Docking and molecular dynamics generated structural hypotheses for PFOA/PFOS–protein interactions, including HMOX1. Conclusions This integrative systems-immunology framework delineates immune–inflammatory signatures at the PFAS–COPD interface and identifies HMOX1, JUNB, CXCL12, and PTGS2 as convergent molecular nodes. The findings do not establish exposure causality but provide a multi-scale basis for future epidemiological and mechanistic studies of how persistent environmental pollutants may contribute to COPD-associated immune and inflammatory alterations.

Single-cell multi-omics reveals cellular atlases and cell–cell interaction networks in metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD)

Linshuang Dou, Wenying Qi, Jinyu Li, 田延豹, Yizi Ao — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fphar.2026.1941671

Objective To summarize recent advances in single-cell multi-omics technologies in metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD), with a focus on cellular atlases, cellular heterogeneity, and intercellular communication networks in hepatic and extrahepatic tissues. Methods This narrative review synthesizes findings from studies identified primarily through PubMed searches supplemented by manual curation of reference lists. Search terms included “single-cell,” “spatial transcriptomics,” “MASLD,” “NAFLD,” “NASH,” “MASH,” “steatohepatitis,” and “liver fibrosis.” Studies applying single-cell transcriptomics, single-nucleus RNA sequencing, spatial transcriptomics, single-cell eQTL analysis, and integrative multi-omics approaches were included. The last literature search was conducted in July 2026. Results: Single-cell technologies have revealed key cellular alterations during MASLD progression, including disrupted hepatocyte metabolic zonation, heterogeneous activation states of hepatic stellate cells, macrophage functional reprogramming, immune cell dysfunction, and dynamic remodeling of the hepatic microenvironment. Spatial transcriptomics has further resolved the spatial organization of these cellular changes, whereas single-cell eQTL analyses have linked genetic susceptibility loci to cell-specific regulatory programs. Single-cell profiling of intestinal, adipose, and vascular tissues has expanded the understanding of extrahepatic contributions and gut–liver communication in MASLD. These advances provide a foundation for drug target identification, mechanistic understanding of current and investigational therapies, and assessment of treatment response at cellular resolution. Conclusion Single-cell multi-omics is transforming MASLD research from tissue-level characterization toward cellular-resolution understanding, providing novel insights into disease stratification, therapeutic target discovery, and biomarker development, and advancing precision hepatology.

B cell–macrophage interactions in colorectal cancer: state coupling, spatiotemporal remodeling, and therapeutic implications

Zhang Zhang, Yi Zhang, Jingjie Lin, Bohao Cheng — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fimmu.2026.2023316

The immune microenvironment of colorectal cancer (CRC) is characterized by pronounced cellular-state and spatial heterogeneity. B cells and macrophages bridge adaptive and innate immunity, respectively, and engage in bidirectional regulation through antibodies and immune complexes, BAFF/APRIL signaling, cytokines, chemokines, and metabolic cues. These interactions are further shaped by tumor cells, stromal components, microorganisms, and other immune populations within the tumor microenvironment. From the perspective of B cell–macrophage state coupling, this review systematically summarizes the biological foundations and reciprocal communication mechanisms underlying their interactions, with particular emphasis on their dynamic remodeling across different disease stages, tertiary lymphoid structures, distinct spatial niches within tumors, and metastatic progression. We further evaluate how established therapies and emerging experimental interventions influence this cellular network. Current evidence suggests that specific B-cell and macrophage states may form distinct functional combinations within either immune-supportive or immunosuppressive ecosystems. However, direct causal evidence remains limited, and the reproducibility of these associations across independent patient cohorts has yet to be firmly established. Future studies should integrate single-cell sequencing, B-cell receptor clonotype tracking, spatial multi-omics, and functional perturbation approaches to move beyond cellular co-occurrence and computationally inferred communication toward direct validation of functional dependencies. Such efforts will also be essential for determining whether composite state–spatial signatures can improve patient stratification, therapeutic response prediction, and treatment decision-making.

Pollutant-induced activation of the AHR–NF-κB–C/EBPβ axis in colorectal cancer

Chao Han, 赵艳, Yang Qin, Hongyuan Yu, Yangyang Yu — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41698-026-01755-w

This study identified environmental pollutants associated with activation of the AHR–NF-κB–C/EBPβ signaling axis and examined their effects in colorectal cancer (CRC). ToxCast indicators and TCGA-COAD/READ RNA-seq data were integrated to construct a pollutant-response matrix; an SVM model prioritized phenmedipham for mechanistic testing. In vitro, phenmedipham increased AHR, p-p65, and C/EBPβ expression and pro-inflammatory cytokine secretion, whereas pathway-component silencing attenuated these effects. In vivo, phenmedipham increased colonic tumor burden and histopathological damage; pathway inhibition mitigated these phenotypes. Immunological analyses indicated impaired dendritic-cell activation and T-cell effector responses. These results provide preclinical evidence that phenmedipham can modulate inflammatory signaling and tumor-associated phenotypes in the tested models. They do not establish human carcinogenicity, CRC risk, or population-level exposure–disease causality; epidemiological validation is required.

Integrative Multiomics Analysis of the Angiogenesis-EMT Interface in Head and Neck Squamous Cell Carcinoma

Sreyashi Das, Elamathi Natarajan, Rashmi Singh, Rizwan Shaikh — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.11.05.764737

Angiogenesis and epithelial-mesenchymal transition (EMT) are key hallmarks that contribute to the progression of oral squamous cell carcinoma (OSCC), but whether the two programs vary simultaneously across tumors and how much of this reflects tumor composition remain unclear. We scored predefined, non-overlapping 20-gene angiogenesis and EMT signatures with a rank-based method in 342 primary oral cavity tumors from The Cancer Genome Atlas (TCGA-OSCC). The two programs were strongly correlated (Spearman rho = 0.809, P = 2.7 x 10^-80), and 2,396 bridge genes were associated with both programs across the transcriptome. The bridge genes were enriched in pathways associated with focal adhesion and extracellular matrix-receptor interaction. In the cellular case, fibroblast and endothelial marker scores were prominent and correlated with the program scores. The coupling was attenuated but remained positive after joint adjustment for tumor purity and stromal and immune scores (partial rho = 0.406) and with an epithelial-restricted EMT score (rho = 0.710). Further correlation was performed with a partial EMT (pEMT), which shows a significant positive correlation (rho = 0.59) and an inverse correlation with tumor purity (rho = -0.70). 146 final gene candidates were found using multi-omic evidence and candidate-level testing on the external cohort GSE30784; after adjusting for inferred cell fractions, 101 of these candidates supported the dual program. For thorough characterization, three candidates with an emphasis on OSMR, MICAL2, and LHFPL2 were chosen. Additional CPTAC data were used to confirm protein-level support, which was strongest for MICAL2. No significant connection was seen between the Interface score and overall survival (hazard ratio per 1-SD increase, 0.92; 95% CI, 0.78-1.08). These results describe a coordinated angiogenesis-EMT transcriptional state in OSCC that is attenuated but retained after accounting for tumor composition, and nominate candidate genes for experimental study.

Plasmacytoid dendritic cells and M2 macrophages are associated with clinical outcomes in human papillomavirus–independent vulvar squamous cell carcinoma

Núria Peñuelas, Lia Sisuashvili, Lorena Marimón, Laia Díez-Ahijado, Núria Carreras‐Dieguez — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fimmu.2026.1922286

Background Vulvar squamous cell carcinoma (VSCC) is a rare malignancy comprising HPV-associated (HPV-A) and HPV-independent (HPV-I) subtypes. HPV-I is associated with high recurrence rates and poor survival, yet lacks validated prognostic biomarkers and therapeutic stratification tools. Although immunotherapy has transformed cancer care and highlighted the tumor microenvironment (TME) as an important determinant of clinical outcome, the rarity of VSCC has limited its molecular characterization, and the TME remains poorly understood. We therefore aimed to define the VSCC TME in an HPV-specific context and identify immune features associated with clinical outcomes. Methods We analyzed 70 surgically treated VSCC (18 HPV-A and 52 HPV-I) using targeted transcriptomic profiling (HTG EdgeSeq). Immune and stromal cell composition was inferred by computational deconvolution, primarily with xCell and supported by EPIC, quanTIseq and MCPcounter. TME composition was compared by HPV status and, within HPV-I VSCC, by p53 and PD-L1 immunohistochemical status. Associations with recurrence-free and disease-specific survival were assessed using Firth-penalized Cox regression adjusted for FIGO stage and p53 status, and candidate populations were evaluated by CD123 and CD163 immunohistochemistry. Results HPV-A tumors showed higher immune and overall microenvironment scores, with enrichment of diverse lymphoid and myeloid populations, whereas HPV-I tumors showed lower immune-cell enrichment. Within HPV-I VSCC, plasmacytoid dendritic cell (pDC) enrichment showed the most consistent association with favorable survival across deconvolution-based analyses, with immunohistochemistry confirming CD123 + cell infiltration as an independent favorable prognostic factor. PD-L1 positivity occurred in 44% of HPV-I tumors and was associated with CD163 + macrophage infiltration, which, in turn, was independently associated with poor outcomes. Conclusions TME profiling identified two clinically relevant immune populations within HPV-I VSCC: CD123 + cell infiltration was associated with favorable outcomes, whereas CD163 + macrophage infiltration was associated with PD-L1 positivity and poorer outcomes. These findings provide a rationale for further investigation of the TME as a source of prognostic and potentially therapeutic biomarkers. Validation in larger, independent cohorts is required before clinical utility can be established.

OmicsBridge: A Shiny-based Interface for Bridging Omics Datasets and Exploring Clinical Relevance

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Hitoshi Matsuo, Mohamed A.A. Mahmoud, Zhaoqing Hu, Cecilia Berzain Battioni, Chong Sun — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1093/bioadv/vbag340

Abstract Motivation Comprehensive omics datasets are now routinely generated across diverse biological conditions, including in laboratories without dedicated bioinformatics support. When integrated and analyzed collectively, these datasets can uncover biologically meaningful relationships and molecular targets with potential clinical relevance. However, efficient integration and exploration of these datasets often require advanced computational workflows. Many existing tools are optimized for single-omics analysis or require users to re-upload datasets for each session, which becomes increasingly time-consuming and inefficient as dataset accumulate. Results We developed OmicsBridge, an intuitive and user-friendly platform for integrating and visualizing diverse omics datasets. OmicsBridge supports over 50 visualization and analysis modules, that help researchers identify genes with functional and clinical relevance and generate hypotheses. It also serves as a centralized database for efficient data storage and retrieval, reducing repetitive data uploads and improving data accessibility and workflow efficiency. As a case study, we used OmicsBridge to identify pathophysiologically relevant molecular determinants of T-cell chemoattractant CXCL10 expression, explore the underlying mechanisms, and assess their association with cancer patient outcomes through multi-omics integration. Availability and implementation OmicsBridge is freely available and platform-independent. A demo web interface is accessible at https://omicsbridge.dkfz.de, and the R/Shiny source code for local deployment is available at https://github.com/htsmto/OmicsBridge.

Recover cell-type composition in held-out experiments — processed data (Galileo Hackathon Track 1)

Yimin Liu — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.24021715

# Recover cell-type composition in held-out experiments — processed data Processed data for a Galileo Hackathon (SIDERIUS) Track 1 challenge: estimate, for every spot of a spatial-transcriptomics-like experiment, the fraction of its cells that belong to each cell type, given a labelled single-cell reference from the same experiment (different cells). ## Files | File | Content | |---|---| | `train.npz` | training experiments: source datasets `brain_cortex`, `kidney` (4 abundance regimes each, 1,500 spots per experiment), with truth | | `validation.npz` | validation experiments: `cerebellum_cell`, `cerebellum_nucleus` (same layout), with truth | | `test_inputs.npz` | test experiments: `hippocampus`, `scc_p5` — inputs only, **no truth** | | `manifest.json` | provenance, split, generation parameters, per-dataset cell counts, sha256 of every file, sha256 commitments to the undistributed test truth and test seed | | `build_dataset.py` | the generator that produced the three .npz files from the converted source matrices | | `01_rds_to_mtx.R` | the converter from the source Seurat `.rds` files to MatrixMarket | All `.npz` files load with `numpy.load(path, allow_pickle=False)`. Per source dataset `d`: `d/types`, `d/genes` (5,000 genes selected from the reference cells only, in selection order; the first 1,000 are the default panel), the labelled reference cells as CSR raw counts (`d/ref_data`, `d/ref_indices`, `d/ref_indptr`, `d/ref_shape`, cells x genes), `d/ref_labels` (index into `d/types`) and `d/ref_barcodes`. Per experiment `e = <dataset>__<regime>`: spot counts as CSR (`e/spot_*`, spots x genes), `e/dataset`, `e/regime`, and in train/validation the truth `e/fractions` (cell-number fraction per type) and `e/cell_counts`. `experiments` and `datasets` list the contents of each file. ## How the data were made 1. Source: the **silver-standard reference** single-cell datasets of the Spotless benchmark (`reference/silver_standard_{1,2,3,4,5,6}*.rds` in `standards.tar.gz` of Zenodo record 10.5281/zenodo.8211492). These are the halves Spotless used as deconvolution references. `silver_standard_5_kidney_pseudosample.rds` was not used. The Spotless gold standards and the shipped Spotless synthetic spots were **not used** (the gold-standard members were never extracted). 2. For each source dataset, cells were split 50/50 per cell type into a reference pool and a generation pool (disjoint). The supplied reference is the reference pool, capped at 500 cells per type. 3. Genes were chosen from the reference pool only (per-type log-CPM fold change versus the other types, detection >= 10% in the type, round robin over types). 4. New mixtures were generated from the generation pool: 2-10 cells per spot; regimes `balanced` (Dirichlet(1) over all types), `sparse` (1-3 types), `dominant` (one type with prior weight 0.7), `rare` (one type present as a single cell in about 20% of spots). Summed counts are binomially thinned to at most a target depth drawn from a log-normal with median 15,000; spots already below the target are left unthinned. Cells are sampled with replacement only when a type's generation pool is smaller than the request (tiny pools, e.g. kidney `novel2` with 3 cells), so a spot can contain the same cell twice. Truth = cell-number fraction per type. 5. Split by whole source group (no group crosses splits): train = brain cortex, kidney; validation = cerebellum (single-cell and single-nucleus, jointly preprocessed by Spotless, kept together); test = hippocampus, human SCC. Training and validation use the public seed 20261106. The test experiments use a secret seed held by the challenge author, so the published generator cannot *regenerate* the test set; `manifest.json` records the sha256 of the test truth file and of the seed. ## Known limitation: test truth is approximately recoverable The test truth is not distributed, but it is derived from public cells. The spots are thinned sums of cells from the public Spotless silver reference files, so fitting each spot against individual public source cells recovers the composition approximately. In independent review on 100 spots per experiment, this gave TV error about 0.017 for hippocampus and 0.10 for SCC, versus about 0.23 and 0.31 for the reference-mean NNLS baseline. This is a known shortcut: the challenge rules forbid using upstream Spotless or source-atlas data, but evaluation cannot prevent it. ## License and attribution The data in this record are a **modified derivative** of "Benchmark datasets for Spotless" by Chananchida Sang-aram (Zenodo, 10.5281/zenodo.8211492, version 0.3, licensed **CC-BY-4.0**, https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/), which accompanies: > Sang-aram C, Browaeys R, Seurinck R, Saeys Y. Spotless, a reproducible pipeline > for benchmarking cell type deconvolution in spatial transcriptomics. eLife > 2024;12:RP88431. doi:10.7554/eLife.88431 Modifications: reference cells re-split into disjoint reference/generation pools; gene subsetting; new synthetic mixtures and truth generated by `build_dataset.py`; repackaged as `.npz`. The data are distributed under **CC-BY-4.0**; please cite the Spotless dataset and paper, and the original studies of each source dataset as listed by Spotless: mouse neocortex, Tasic et al. 2018 (GEO GSE115746, Allen Institute); mouse cerebellum single-cell, Saunders et al. 2018 (GEO GSE116470); mouse cerebellum single-nucleus, Kozareva et al. 2021 (GEO GSE165371); mouse hippocampus, Zeisel et al. 2018 (Linnarsson Lab Mouse Brain Atlas); mouse kidney, Park et al. 2018 (GEO GSE107585); human cutaneous squamous cell carcinoma patient 5, Ji et al. 2020 (GEO GSE144236). The original studies' own terms also apply. The code files in this record (`build_dataset.py`, `01_rds_to_mtx.R`) are released under the MIT license.

A metabolic state in the cancer-associated fibroblast differentiation continuum of colorectal cancer

Jiajie Zhou — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fcell.2026.1994563

Background The metabolic heterogeneity of cancer-associated fibroblasts (CAFs) in the colorectal cancer (CRC) microenvironment remains poorly understood. Methods We profiled 86,142 cells from two independent CRC cohorts (GSE132465, GSE144735) using single-cell RNA-sequencing and validated findings in a public Visium dataset (GSE285505, 3,493 spots) and an external spatial cohort (GSE267401, 4 sections). Unsupervised clustering identified CAF subtypes, regulon analysis (SCENIC) mapped transcriptional drivers, and spatial deconvolution (cell2location) localized CAF populations in tissue. Findings Unsupervised clustering of 13,003 CAFs identified a metabolically distinct subpopulation (meCAF, 4.1% of CAFs) that scored highest for glycolysis (Δ = +0.60, P < 1e-4), lactate metabolism (Δ = +0.46, P < 1e-4) and hypoxia among nine metabolic pathways compared across all three CAF subtypes. SCENIC analysis inferred HIF1A as the regulon with the highest meCAF specificity (ΔAUC = +0.111, 95% CI 0.081 to 0.134, P = 2.4e-07 by patient-paired Wilcoxon test, 23/23 patients concordant). Spatial profiling confirmed meCAF enrichment at the tumor-stroma interface with spatial concordance with lactate metabolism (Spearman ρ = 0.29–0.75). meCAF abundance was inversely correlated with distance to the cancer epithelium (ρ = −0.317, p < 3e-82), accompanied by a metabolic activity gradient. In TCGA-COADREAD (n = 410), the meCAF signature varied significantly across consensus molecular subtypes (ANOVA p = 1.9e-18), with the highest scores in CMS1 (immune subtype) and the lowest in CMS2 (epithelial subtype). Interpretation These findings reveal a metabolically distinct CAF subpopulation operating within spatially restricted periepithelial micro-niches, with its clinical association to the immune-rich CMS1 subtype suggesting that stromal metabolic state may relate to the immune context of CRC.

Mapping the prediction-invisible fraction of an engineered immunopeptidome: a pilot analysis of immunoproteasome-reprogrammed mesenchymal stromal cells

Riam Shammaa, Jamilah Abusarah, Moutih Rafei, Nehmé El-Hachem — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fimmu.2026.1997527

Algorithms that predict which peptides the immune system sees are central to cancer vaccine design, yet they are trained almost entirely on binding affinity and remain largely blind to how the antigen-processing machinery shapes the repertoire that reaches the cell surface. Mesenchymal stromal cells engineered to express the immunoproteasome (MSC-IPr) are a whole-cell vaccine platform that presents a larger and chemically distinct peptide repertoire than dendritic cells. Here we use a pilot immunopeptidomics dataset from that platform to ask how much of a processing-reprogrammed repertoire current prediction models can see. Of 234 peptides detected only after tumour-lysate pulsing, 34 (14.5%) were empirically eluted from MHC class I yet scored as non-binders by NetMHCpan-4.1, MHCflurry affinity and MHCflurry presentation simultaneously. This is twenty-fold the rate obtained by applying identical criteria to an independently generated C57BL/6 immunopeptidome atlas (0.65%). These peptides are weak binders rather than true non-binders, and an orthogonal immunogenicity model ranks them modestly above the predictable majority. They are associated with a candidate non-canonical anchor pattern: an aspartate at P2 that is positional rather than compositional (3.4-fold above a composition-preserving null) and absent from catalogued H2-Db epitope space. Because the dataset comprises single mass-spectrometry runs without a proteasome-matched control, we present these as pilot observations, not established effects. We propose the engineered-processing strategy they illustrate, in which cells with defined, non-canonical processing serve as an empirical filter for epitope discovery, and we specify the replication, spectral-validation and control arms required to test it.

Modular Connections: The Connections Between Residual Streams Can Themselves Be Modular Models

Yue Su — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.24032653

Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections (mHC) widen the residual stream of a Transformer into N parallel streams and, at every sublayer, generate per-token coefficients that decide which streams to read from, which streams to write to, and how the streams mix. I show that once the N streams are interleaved, these three operations are exactly a convolution and a deconvolution with kernel size and stride N, and a sliding N×N map, so the connections of each token are simply a set of kernels. Building on this view, I propose Modular Connections (MoC): every sublayer keeps banks of read, write and mixing experts, and each token is routed to a few of them, as in a mixture of experts, and combines them with weights. This design matches a recent analysis of DeepSeek-V4-Flash, whose trained mHC settles on fairly fixed connection patterns per sublayer with little per-token variation. Because the mixing matrices of MoC are expert parameters rather than per-token outputs, their Sinkhorn normalization can be precomputed after training. In my measurements on a 1.36B-parameter model, the MoC forward pass is 2.2–3.2× faster than that of mHC on single sequences without compilation and about 1.5× faster on short inputs once compiled, the training step compiles about 16× faster, and routing can be inspected expert by expert. In an ongoing 49B-token run, the validation bits-per-byte of MoC has stayed within −0.0009 to +0.0017 of mHC at matching steps from step 2,000 to step 6,360. Web version with interactive animations: https://selen-suyue.github.io/modular-connections/en/

SpatialROI: An Interactive R Shiny Platform for Customized Region-Aware Spatial Transcriptomics Analysis

Mengyao Lu, Aodong Qiu, Min Xu, Xinghua Lu, Lujia Chen — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1093/bioadv/vbag339

Abstract Motivation Spatial transcriptomics captures high-throughput molecular information across tissue sections, but histological context remains essential for accurate biological insight. Clustering-derived spatial domains are sensitive to technical noise and may not align with morphologically defined structures that pathologists rely on for disease interpretation. Expert-guided region definition directly on H&E images enables precise identification of molecular signals within tissue architecture. However, existing tools require substantial programming expertise or lack seamless integration between image-based region selection and downstream transcriptomic analysis, limiting accessibility for non-computational users. Results We present SpatialROI, an interactive platform that enables users to define regions of interest (ROIs) directly on H&E images and perform targeted analyses without programming. SpatialROI supports standard 10x Visium data as both raw Space Ranger outputs and preprocessed Seurat objects. The platform incorporates eight analytical modules: ROI drawing, gene set and pathway visualization, ligand–receptor colocalization, cell-type deconvolution, spot clustering, differential expression, within-sample group comparison, and multi-sample ROI comparison. ROIs are exportable as Seurat objects or reusable spot-index files. Collectively, SpatialROI facilitates expert-guided, region-aware exploration of spatial transcriptomic data for biologically meaningful insights into tissue architecture. Availability and implementation SpatialROI is freely accessible at https://shiny.crc.pitt.edu/spatial_api/. Source code and example datasets are available at https://github.com/myaol/SpatialROI.

Adaptive selection of MICE algorithm parameters for pulmonary function value prediction models

Seongjae Im, Yeonghui Gang, Myung-Mo Lee, Jucheol Moon, Hongjun Kim — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-026-78761-2

Abstract Artificial intelligence-based medical prediction models are often affected by incomplete datasets containing missing values, which can significantly reduce model reliability and predictive performance. Among various imputation approaches, Multiple Imputation by Chained Equations (MICE) is widely used in medical data analysis because it models each variable using the conditional distributions of the remaining variables while reflecting uncertainty in the imputation process. Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) is a chronic respiratory disorder characterized by persistent airflow limitation, requiring early diagnosis and continuous monitoring for improved prognosis. In this study, a pulmonary function test dataset collected from 598 subjects over a two-year period was used to investigate the effects of time-series sampling and missing-data imputation on prediction performance. An adaptive selection method was proposed for the initialization strategy and maximum iteration count of the MICE algorithm based on the statistical characteristics of representative pulmonary function indicators, including FEV1, FVC, FEF25–75%, and PEF. Different missing rates were artificially introduced, and the performances of Piecewise Cubic Hermite Interpolating Polynomial (PCHIP) and multivariate iterative regression-based MICE were compared. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed adaptive MICE strategy maintains stable predictive performance under increasing missing rates and provides reliable predictive information by effectively reflecting multivariate correlation structures.

The gut–brain–immune axis in aging and neurodegenerative diseases: evidence and candidate mechanisms linking dance-based exercise with neuroplasticity, neuroinflammation, immune homeostasis, and cognitive resilience

Jin Liu, Tianyu Jia, Wenli Yao — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s12979-026-00602-7

Aging is associated with progressive changes in immune regulation, brain function, metabolic homeostasis, and physical performance, which in turn increases susceptibility to neurodegenerative disorders and diminishes quality of life. Emerging evidence indicates that the gut–brain–immune axis is a key biological interface through which lifestyle interventions may influence age-related physiological changes. Dance-based interventions—complex multisystem stimuli that integrate aerobic exercise, motor coordination, cognitive involvement, social engagement, and emotional expression—have attracted growing interest as potential components of healthy aging. This review examines the evidence for and candidate mechanisms linking dance-based exercise with the gut–brain–immune axis, immune homeostasis, neuroplasticity, and cognitive resilience. Particular attention is given to microbiome-related pathways, microbiota-derived metabolites, inflammatory and immune signaling, mitochondrial and redox regulation, and neural mechanisms relevant to cognitive and emotional function. We distinguish direct evidence from human dance interventions from evidence derived from broader exercise research and preclinical mechanistic studies, and evaluate their potential relevance to aging and neurodegenerative diseases. While existing evidence supports potential biological and functional benefits of dance-based interventions, direct evidence for dance-induced microbiome remodeling and its causal contribution to neuroimmune or cognitive outcomes remains limited. Thus, microbiome mediation of dance-associated neuroimmune and cognitive effects has not yet been established. Additional longitudinal, multi-omics, and mechanistically integrated studies are needed to clarify these relationships and determine whether gut–brain–immune pathways contribute to the effects of dance-based exercise during aging.

Multi-omics analysis reveals microbial and metabolic signatures associated with neutral detergent fiber digestibility in dairy cattle

Boyan Ma, Jianxin Xiao, Yiming Xu, Tianyu Chen, Shuai Liu — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s40104-026-01519-3

BACKGROUND: The rumen microbiome is central to fiber degradation in dairy cows, yet the precise functional and metabolic networks driving variations in neutral detergent fiber (NDF) digestibility remain poorly understood. Elucidating these microbiome-host interactions is critical for improving forage utilization and advancing sustainable dairy production. RESULTS: Employing an integrative metagenomics-metabolomics approach, we characterized the rumen microecology of lactating dairy cows exhibiting high and low NDF digestibility phenotypes (n = 10/group). Metagenomic profiling revealed a significant enrichment of fibrolytic taxa (Prevotella sp., Lachnospiraceae bacterium) and carbohydrate-active enzymes in the high-NDF group (HI). In addition, species-level differential abundance testing showed that Methanobrevibacter spp. was more abundant in the low-NDF group (LO) than in the HI group, whereas no archaeal differences were detected at the phylum or genus levels. Functional and metabolomic analyses indicated broader enrichment of amino acid degradation functions and higher concentrations of major volatile fatty acids (VFAs) in HI cows, consistent with stronger overall ruminal fermentation activity. Furthermore, VFAs and microbial composition explained 54.3% and 43.0% of the variation in NDF digestibility, respectively. An exploratory machine learning model based on selected annotated metabolite features showed moderate discriminatory performance, with an out-of-bag accuracy of 77.19%. CONCLUSIONS: Our findings suggest that coordinated shifts in rumen microbial composition, amino acid metabolism, and VFA production are associated with variation in NDF digestibility in dairy cattle. These results provide a basis for future studies aimed at improving fiber utilization through microbiome-informed nutritional or breeding strategies.

Burcu Karpuz Seren — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003588818-44

Management of non-motor symptoms such as headache and sleep disturbances in neurodegenerative diseases requires consideration of individual genetic and environmental variability, beyond classical therapeutic approaches. Precision medicine has rapidly advanced through multi-omic strategies, including genomics, transcriptomics, proteomics, and metabolomics, as well as digital health technologies and artificial intelligence (AI)–based analytics. In clinical decision-making, biomarker-driven algorithms and AI-assisted digital monitoring systems provide high effectiveness in early diagnosis and risk stratification. SARDINE unlocks EWAS summary statistics for large-scale epigenetic biomarker analysis

Yuchang Wu, Yushan Wang, Ryan Moreno, Sarah M. Merrill, Varun B. Dwaraka — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.11.05.763758

Epigenetic clocks are increasingly used to study biological aging, yet their interpretation and utility remain uncertain. Meanwhile, the growing wealth of epigenome-wide association study (EWAS) summary statistics remains largely untapped for evaluating these biomarkers. Here, we introduce SARDINE, a statistical framework that estimates epigenetic clock-phenotype associations using EWAS summary statistics, clock weights, and reference methylation covariance, without individual-level data. SARDINE accurately reproduced individual-level association estimates across 38 epigenetic biomarkers and four phenotypes in 15,677 individuals. Applying SARDINE to diverse EWAS datasets revealed substantial heterogeneity in biomarker associations with aging, diseases, and lifestyle factors. By transforming CpG-level EWAS results into interpretable biomarker-level associations, SARDINE unlocks a largely underutilized data resource and establishes a scalable paradigm for systematic biomarker evaluation and discovery in aging research.

Defining the progression of normal and non-progression humeral shaft fracture healing using the plasma proteome

Louis C. Gerstenfeld, Paul Tornetta, Serkalem Demisse, Nazanin Nafisi, Avani Vaishnav — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-026-74324-7

ABSTRACT Current diagnostic tools are neither informative of, nor concurrent with the underlying biological processes of fracture healing. We examined if there would be protein markers in the plasma of non-operatively fixed, isolated, non-articular, humeral fractures that would be informative of these biological processes. Five follow-up visits over a six-month period were examined in 24 patients that healed (union) and 7 non-healers (nonunion). Using the first or last clinic visit as the reference, 118 proteins out of ∼7000 proteins that were assayed, showed differential expression at log 2 fold change ≥1.3 fold and a significance of FDR ≤ 0.5. Multiple proteins related to endochondral bone development showed significant increased plasma levels during the first 12-16 weeks. Proteins associated with acute phase response, inflammation and lipid storage showed decreased levels after their initial induction. Using a time point to time point comparison for the first three follow-up visits (up to 9 weeks after injury) between healed and nonunion patients, identified 41 proteins with differential levels at p ≤ 0.05 significance, with ten overlapping the 118 proteins that tracked normal healing. The differential group associated with the nonunion patient‘s included specific extracellular matrix and acute phase proteins that showed decreased levels relative to normal healers. These data establish the progression of endochondral bone formation and place them in context to radiological and functional outcomes in human fracture healing. The identification of differentially expressed proteins in nonunion plasma suggests the potential for the development of diagnostic markers for the early intervention of nonunion. PINN-based functional inference in size-structured population models: Application to fish oogenesis

Louis Fostier, Manon Lesage, Violette Thermes, Frédérique Clément, Romain Yvinec — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pcbi.1014933

Early oogenesis in juvenile fish establishes the ovarian reserve, which conditions lifelong reproductive capacity. This process is regulated by local hormonal feedback mechanisms, mainly involving Anti-Müllerian Hormone (AMH). In this work, we develop a mechanistic size-structured population model describing an unstructured germ-cell compartment and a population of growing oocytes, with AMH feedback on the renewal of the precursor cells compartment. Using repeated cross-sectional observations of oocyte size distributions in fish ovaries, we formulate and solve a nonlinear inverse problem with Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) to infer three demographic functions: the size-dependent oocyte growth rate, the AMH-regulated net renewal rate of the precursor cells compartment, and the recruitment rate of new oocytes. We introduce an auxiliary PINN formulation to handle the nonlocal interaction term and jointly infer unobserved state variables and demographic functions. Our inference strategy combines non-parametric PINNs with a data-guided model-selection step: demographic functions are first inferred without prescribing their functional forms, and the learned profiles are then used to select parsimonious parametric laws, whose parameters are subsequently estimated within a parametric PINN. We investigate identifiability at both the functional and parametric levels, combining structural identifiability analysis with synthetic-data experiments assessing the practical recovery of functions and parameters. The inference procedure is embedded within an individual-level bootstrap framework to assess sensitivity to repeated-cross-sectional sampling and stochastic training. Applied to juvenile fish oogenesis, the framework recovers interpretable growth and renewal dynamics, provides access to unobserved quantities, and enables conditional in silico investigation. More broadly, this work provides a framework for functional and parametric inference in size-structured population models with nonlocal interactions from sparse cross-sectional data.

Experimental and theoretical investigation of parameter-driven stability and chaos in a non-linear neuron circuit model

Haiqa Ehsan, Haseeba Sajjad, Adil Jhangeer, Lubomı́r Řı́ha — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0360729

In this study, a neural-inspired electronic circuit consisting of a pressure-sensitive FitzHugh-Nagumo model with the addition of a piezoelectric bi-membrane and two capacitors is explored. The governing equations are obtained analytically from Kirchhoff's laws, which represent the nonlinear interactions, oscillatory response, and stability of the circuit. A perturbation term is added to investigate the deviations from the system potential, and the transitions in the system are studied under the assumption of the model. By applying statistical methods and eigenvalue analysis, the parameter space is systematically subdivided and examined, and the results show that the behavior of the system is dominated by divergent behavior, although some periodic oscillatory behavior is seen in certain regions. This highlights the importance of initial conditions and parameter selection on the behavior of the system, which are essential to obtaining stable behavior and developing an efficient control design. Furthermore, an ensemble-size sensitivity analysis is performed to verify the robustness and convergence of the statistical characterization across different numbers of initial conditions. Phase disturbance plots, power spectrum density plots, attractor reconstruction plots, return maps, and noise detection maps are used to analyze the system dynamics. In addition, a multi-sum (multi-parameter interaction) diagram is created to depict the interactions between circuit variables and the overall system behavior.

TC-Flow: Trajectory-Coupled Multimodal Flow Matching for Incomplete Modality Learning

Haoran Meng, Lisi Mo, Yifan Fang, Ke Qin, Guiduo Duan — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1145/3767308.3835736

Multimodal models often suffer severe performance degradation due to missing modalities during real-world inference. Although diffusion models are widely adopted for missing modality completion, they exhibit a counter-intuitive paradox: their high-fidelity reconstruction barely improves downstream accuracy, yielding a <1.3% gain over pure Gaussian noise under extreme missingness. We define this as the Terminal-State Supervision Gap: constrained by intermediate denoising optimization, existing models lack direct task-level constraints on final features, while the inherent variance of multi-step sampling triggers downstream error amplification. To bridge this gap, we propose the Trajectory-Coupled Multimodal Flow (TC-Flow) framework. Specifically, the core Prediction-Coupled Flow Dynamics (PCFD) module combines Conditional Flow Matching and x-prediction to directly predict terminal features, while employing an Ordinary Differential Equation (ODE) solver to backpropagate downstream task losses end-to-end, explicitly shaping generation dynamics with discriminative objectives. Furthermore, the Trajectory-Calibrated Fusion (TCF) module extracts the dynamic stability of the ODE trajectory as a reliability prior to adaptively calibrate fusion gating, proactively blocking error cascades from unreliable completions. Extensive experiments on CMU-MOSI, CMU-MOSEI, and Weibo-17 demonstrate that TC-Flow consistently outperforms existing methods across various missing protocols, achieving up to an 8.0% absolute performance boost on CMU-MOSEI under the fixed-missing protocol. Code and trained models will be released upon publication.

Generalized Integral Transform Modeling of Complex Coupled-Field Transport in Two-Dimensional Keller–Segel Chemotaxis: Verification, Spectral Convergence, and Self-Similar Localization

Alberto M.G. Tolentino, Kalysta O.R. Borges, Carlos H.R. Moura, Bruno Marques Viegas, Emanuel Negrão Macêdo — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.56578/jcmes020103

Complex engineering systems often involve interacting transport fields in which diffusion, field-driven fluxes, production or decay, and nonlinear feedback jointly control stability, transport, and localization.Although originating in mathematical biology, the Keller-Segel system is a canonical coupled-field transport model with this structure and provides a stringent benchmark for computational methodologies aimed at complex and multiphysics transport problems.A multidimensional hybrid numerical-analytical formulation based on the Generalized Integral Transform Technique (GITT) is developed for a parabolic-parabolic Keller-Segel system with homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions.Spatial averages are treated analytically, zero-mean fields are expanded in normalized Neumann eigenfunctions, and exact cosine-product selection rules generate a sparse nonlinear modal system.The transformed equations are advanced using CVODE, a variable-step, variable-order ordinary differential equation (ODE) solver from the SUNDIALS (Suite of Nonlinear and Differential/Algebraic Equation Solvers) package, with variable-order backward differentiation formulas (BDF), together with a matrix-free scaled preconditioned generalized minimal residual (SPGMR) solver in two dimensions.Systematic one-and two-dimensional convergence studies are performed for chemotactic sensitivities χ = 1-5 and are complemented by independent conservative finite-volume/method-of-lines solutions in physical space.The finite-volume discretizations exhibit second-order self-convergence and closely reproduce the converged GITT fields.The parametric study quantifies the transition from weak relaxation to strong aggregation and the growth of genuinely two-dimensional mixed modes.For χ = 5, successive truncations up to 7,920 spatial modes (15,840 coupled ODEs) resolve a strongly localized regime.Physical and spectral localization scales contract consistently, while normalized profiles collapse onto an approximately invariant shape.Independent late-time extrapolations yield characteristic times clustered near t ≈ 2.184.The results demonstrate a high-resolution hybrid framework for nonlinear coupled-field partial differential equation (PDE) systems with cross-field transport, evolving localization, and demanding multiscale resolution, while providing numerical evidence compatible with possible finite-time singularization without constituting a mathematical proof of blow-up.

Mathematical Modeling on the Effect of Flood on Farmland: A Nonlinear Dynamical Systems Approach

Godspower Chukwunedum Abanum, Innocent Cleopas Eli — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.25259/jqus_32_2026

Objectives This paper provides a three-dimensional nonlinear ordinary differential equation (ODE) model to clearly understand the effects of flooding on agricultural farmlands. The model uses three interacting state variables: farmland productivity F(t), soil quality index S(t), and floodwater level W(t). The novelty of this work lies in: (i) explicit mechanistic coupling between all three variables, including positive feedback from soil quality to productivity and negative feedback from vegetation to floodwater (ii) identification of critical bifurcation thresholds using rigorous stability analysis; and (iii) quantification of synergistic effects from combined management interventions. Material and Methods Existence and uniqueness of solutions were established using the Picard-Lindelöf theorem; all biologically possible equilibrium points were identified, and their stability was assessed through the Routh-Hurwitz criteria. Results Extensive numerical modeling was carried out using MATLAB and sensitivity analysis was applied to identify the most critical parameters governing system behavior: drainage capacity (δ), flood input intensity (μ), and the crop damage coefficient (α). The model reveals critical thresholds i.e at drainage capacity 0.30 and flood input 0.70, above which the system reaches the irreversible degradation regimes. In combinations, there are synergistic advantages, with up to 32.8% productivity gain recorded when drainage infrastructure, flood-resistant crops, and soil conservation have been integrated into management. Conclusion The results show that thresholds for drainage and flood-input parameters exist that govern the extent of degradation of farmland productivity during flooding, and that integrated management strategies are considerably more effective than single interventions, providing actionable evidence-based targets in agricultural resilience planning in flood-prone areas.

Mathematical modeling and statistical analysis for predicting success in intrauterine insemination, intracytoplasmic sperm injection, and frozen embryo transfer

Somayeh Ghiasi Hafezi, Negar Soleimani, Atena Ghasemabadi, Kosar Namakin, Mark G. Ghamsary — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-026-78865-9

Infertility affects millions of couples worldwide, and assisted reproductive technologies (ART) such as intrauterine insemination (IUI), intracytoplasmic sperm injection (ICSI), and frozen embryo transfer (ET Freeze) are commonly used to treat it. However, the success rates of these methods vary considerably, and the factors that predict successful outcomes are not fully understood. Identifying these predictors is crucial for personalized treatment planning and improving clinical decision-making. This study aims to identify and compare the significant predictors of treatment success across three ART methods: IUI, ICSI, and ET Freeze. We also seek to develop statistical and mathematical models to evaluate the relationship between patient-related factors and pregnancy outcomes, and to assess which model provides the best fit for predicting treatment success. This retrospective cohort study was conducted at Royan Institute, Tehran, Iran, using data from 2011–2014. The unit of analysis was the ART treatment cycle. After data cleaning, 6240 cycles remained. The final ordinal logistic regression analyses included 4592 cycles with a known ordinal outcome (blighted ovum, abortion, or live birth). Repeated cycles from the same couple were accounted for using generalized estimating equations (GEE) with couple ID as the clustering variable. Ordinal logistic regression was used to model the association between predictors and treatment outcome. Of the 6240 participants, 209 underwent IUI, 2598 underwent ICSI, and 1783 underwent ET Freeze. For IUI, significant predictors of reduced success were increasing age (OR 0.94, 95% CI 0.87–1.00, p < 0.058), BMI \(\ge \) 30 kg/m \(^2\) (OR 0.46, 95% CI 0.19–1.10, p \(=\) 0.076), and longer duration of infertility (OR 0.93, 95% CI 0.86–1.02, p = 0.116). For ICSI, significant predictors included age (OR 0.93, 95% CI 0.92–0.95, p < 0.001), BMI \(\ge \) 30 kg/m \(^2\) (OR 0.74, 95% CI 0.58–0.95, p \(=\) 0.018), fertilization rate (OR 1.73, 95% CI 1.21–2.50, p \(=\) 0.003), number of mature oocytes (OR 2.31, 95% CI 1.41–3.83, p \(=\) 0.001), and number of grade A embryos (OR 1.37, 95% CI 1.04–1.82, p \(=\) 0.025). For ET Freeze, age (OR 0.97, 95% CI 0.95–0.99, p \(=\) 0.002) and number of previous abortions (OR 0.77, 95% CI 0.60–0.99, p \(=\) 0.037) were significant negative predictors of success. The predictors of treatment success differ significantly across the three ART methods. For IUI, BMI, duration of infertility, and age were the main predictors. For ICSI, a broader set of factors including BMI, age, number of embryos, fertilization rate, and PCOS were influential. For ET Freeze, only age and number of previous abortions were significant. These findings highlight the importance of patient-specific factors in ART outcomes and suggest that tailored treatment strategies based on individual predictors may improve success rates. The proposed statistical and mathematical models provide valuable tools for clinicians to better counsel patients and optimize treatment selection.

Walsh Analysis in the Twenty-First Century: Convergence, Summability, Nonlinear Approximation, Universality, and Digital Applications

Sergo A. Episkoposian — 2026-11-06 — arxiv

DOI : —

Walsh analysis connects ordered Fourier convergence, summability, nonlinear approximation, and universal representation through a common character algebra. This survey examines developments from 2000-2026 against their classical foundations. Binary kernel geometry explains Hardy-space losses and summability thresholds; interval and packet methods control spectral selection; localized blocks produce correction and universal representations with distinct coefficient and exceptional-set requirements. Selected proofs and counterexamples compare these mechanisms and identify where an implication needs an additional hypothesis. The same distinctions organize digital integration, sparse measurements, Boolean spectra, and generalized Walsh bases. Quantitative thresholds, source conventions, and published theorem pointers are collected in comparative tables. The concluding problems separate source-listed questions, proposed refinements, and elementary obstructions. Supplementary derivations preserve normalization checks and finite covariance calculations while keeping the main exposition focused on the analytic mechanisms.

HyperCD: Effective Source Detection in Hypergraphs via Causal Disentanglement

Yan Kang, Peican Zhu, Keke Tang, Le Cheng, Zun Liu — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1145/3767308.3835654

Source detection in hypergraphs plays an important role in mitigating the spread of harmful rumors and ensuring social stability. Current methods frequently overlook the causal entanglement problem in observations, thereby limiting the performance of source inference. In this work, we propose a source detection approach in Hypergraphs via Causal Disentanglement (HyperCD). To decouple causal propagation semantics from confounding noise, we propose the causal disentanglement module. A dual-branch variational encoder is utilized to disentangle snapshot embeddings into causal and confounding latent spaces, ensuring that the inference module relies solely on causal propagation signals. To further enforce this separation, an adversarial inference loss term is introduced to guarantee statistical independence between the decoupled branches. Moreover, to capture diffusion dynamics in hypergraphs, an attention-based hypergraph aggregation module is designed, which comprises the node-to-hyperedge propagation and hyperedge-to-node propagation processes. Extensive experiments on six datasets demonstrate the superiority of our HyperCD in effectively mitigating the causal entanglement problem.

Molecular and in-silico characterization of vancomycin-resistant Enterococcus spp. isolated from clinical urine samples, Lahore, Pakistan

Nadeem Khan, Mejerrah Aamir, Aatif Amin, Ghazala Saeed, Ghulam Mujtaba — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-026-78349-w

Vancomycin-resistant enterococci are a serious threat to public health because of their multidrug resistance and the limitations in the treatment options available. This study investigated clinical VRE isolates from Lahore, Pakistan, and characterized the vanA gene via in-silico approaches. In this study, urine specimens were collected and cultured on selective media. Morphological and biochemical observation, such as Gram staining and catalase, oxidase, and mannitol salt agar tests, were conducted. Antibiotic susceptibility testing was done via the Kirby-Bauer method. DNA was extracted and the PCR amplified the genomic DNA, vanA , and vanB genes. Protein sequences were obtained from NCBI and analyzed with ProtParam, PSIPRED, and AlphaFold3. olecular docking was carried out using AutoDock Vina, structural dynamics were evaluated with iMODS-based normal mode analysis, and immune simulation conducted with C-ImmSim. Seven clinical VRE isolates were characterized. All isolates showed elevated MICs to vancomycin, ciprofloxacin, and chloramphenicol, meeting CLSI resistance criteria; gentamicin and azithromycin MICs were also elevated but are reported descriptively, as CLSI interpretive breakpoints for these agents against Enterococcus spp. are not established for routine susceptibility categorization. PCR provided evidence for the presence of the vanA gene (732 bp) in all of the isolates. Genomic DNA sequencing of two representative isolates (NK-01, NK-02) yielded partial vanA gene sequence, confirmed via BLASTN analysis against isolate-derived and reference vanA records; these sequences did not provide species-level discrimination, and species identification for all isolates remained at the genus level ( Enterococcus spp.). The VanA protein was relatively stable (aliphatic index 94.69) with a globular fold and showed moderate docking with Vancomycin (− 6.0 kcal/mol). Normal mode analysis showed the domains to be flexible due to low eigenvalues. Immune simulation demonstrated prediction for strong IgM and IgG responses, along with identification of promising B- and T-cell epitopes, though a separate immunogenicity assessment yielded conflicting predictions. The current study confirmed VanA-mediated resistance in multidrug-resistant Enterococcus spp. and gave structural and immunological insight into VanA. These findings support VanA as a potential target for further therapeutic investigation; given that surface exposure, secretion potential, conservation across isolates, and population coverage were not assessed, its vaccine potential remains preliminary and unconfirmed, and the necessity for continued surveillance and development of new inhibitors against VRE is emphasized.

Yue Gao, Jiahui Xu, 李双彦, Wenjuan Yi, Liuyan Xiao — 2026-11-06 — openalex

The subcommissural organ (SCO) is an evolutionarily conserved structure in the brains of vertebrates, but its function remains largely unknown. EPIDEMIOLOGICAL SIGNIFICANCE OF DRINKING WATER AND SANITARY-HYGIENIC FACTORS IN THE TRANSMISSION OF VIRAL HEPATITIS A

Pattojonov Shoxislom Dilmurodbek ugli, Worldly Knowledge Publishing Centre — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.24011396

Viral hepatitis A remains one of the most prominent waterborne and foodborne infectious pathologies worldwide, driven primarily by the fecal-oral mechanism of transmission and tightly interconnected with community sanitary-hygienic infrastructure and drinking water security. The hepatitis A virus possesses exceptional environmental resilience, allowing it to withstand standard chemical disinfection protocols and persist across aquatic ecosystems for extended periods. Although routine epidemiological surveillance typically focuses on retrospective case notification, this approach often fails to capture the complex spatial dynamics, microbiological vulnerabilities of municipal and decentralized water distribution networks, and seasonal patterns that govern communal outbreaks. This comprehensive epidemiological investigation systematically examines the correlation between communal water supply parameters, sanitary infrastructure, and the incidence dynamics of viral hepatitis A. A multi-year observational epidemiological and sanitary-microbiological study was conducted across urban and rural populations exhibiting variable levels of centralized water purification and sewerage infrastructure. Epidemiological profiling encompassed the spatial-temporal mapping of officially registered viral hepatitis A cases, serological identification of anti-hepatitis A immunoglobulin M among symptomatic patients, quantitative microbial risk assessment of municipal tap water and decentralized well sources, and reverse transcription-polymerase chain reaction detection of viral ribonucleic acid in environmental water samples. The findings demonstrate that viral hepatitis A morbidity exhibits pronounced autumn-winter seasonality and a disproportionately high incidence among school-aged children and adolescents. Communities relying on aging, intermittently pressurized distribution piping and decentralized shallow groundwater sources demonstrated a statistically significant increase in hepatitis A incidence relative to regions with continuous chlorination and modernized centralized sanitation. Furthermore, hepatitis A incidence correlated strongly and positively with the microbial contamination indices of drinking water sources—specifically total coliform counts and thermotolerant coliform bacteria—and inversely with residual free chlorine concentrations in municipal networks. The study concludes that municipal drinking water security and modern sanitary-hygienic infrastructure represent the fundamental barriers against waterborne enteric viral transmission, emphasizing that molecular environmental surveillance coupled with strategic vaccination is vital for achieving sustainable epidemiological control.

Metabolite-driven protein post-translational modifications: network mechanisms bridging metabolic sensing and functional regulation

Shuangcui Wang, Yue Zhang — 2026-11-06 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fcell.2026.1984644

Metabolites have traditionally been viewed primarily as energy sources and biosynthetic substrates for cells. However, the discovery of novel protein modifications such as lactylation, succinylation, and O-GlcNAcylation demonstrates that metabolites can also function as signaling molecules, whose activated high-energy derivatives directly drive post-translational modifications (PTMs) of proteins. The regulatory axis of ‘metabolic sensing–PTM remodeling–cellular functional reprogramming’ that emerges from this plays a significant role in the pathogenesis of tumors, immune disorders, and metabolic genetic diseases. This review centers on this regulatory axis. We first elaborate the chemical-structural basis governing metabolite-driven PTM biogenesis, followed by discussion of upstream metabolic-pathway activation and downstream regulatory outputs, including chromatin remodeling, protein phase separation and metabolic feedback circuits. Based on this, we employ cross-species comparisons to discuss the evolutionary conservation and functional divergence of this axis. This analysis clarifies several long-standing controversies, including the distinction between enzymatic and non-enzymatic modifications, the crosstalk between different modifications, the concentration thresholds required in vivo and in vitro for modification occurrence, and the strategies for validating causality. Finally, advances in technologies such as single-cell and spatial multi-omics, live-cell metabolic probes, targeted small-molecule tools, and systems modeling are discussed to explore their potential applications, aiming to provide insights for biomarker discovery and precision intervention in metabolic diseases. Central to this review is the emphasis on chemical and biophysical constraints governing metabolite-driven PTMs, which shape their occurrence, compartment-dependent enzyme-dependence, and downstream biological outputs.
